# Iran Update Evening Special Report, March 5, 2026

*Source: <https://understandingwar.org/research/middle-east/iran-update-evening-special-report-march-5-2026/>*

## Key Takeaways

1. The Israel Defense Forces (IDF) has continued strikes targeting Iranian ballistic missile infrastructure in order to degrade Iranian missile capabilities. The IDF reported on March 5 that Israeli aircraft had struck hundreds of missile launch sites across Iran and rendered over 300 ballistic missile launchers inoperable since February 28. Degrading Iranian air defenses allows US and Israeli aircraft to operate with less risk and greater freedom of operation over Iranian airspace.
2. The US and Israeli combined force has advanced to the next phase of their campaign, which will focus on targeting Iranian defense industrial assets, especially missile production facilities. The first phase of the campaign suppressed Iranian air defenses, decapitated its command and control, and limited its ability to retaliate with ballistic missiles and drones. The IDF issued an evacuation warning on March 5 for the Abbas Abad Industrial Zone and Shenzar Industrial Zone in Pakdasht, Tehran Province, likely in preparation to target Iran’s defense industrial base.
3. The combined force continues to conduct airstrikes targeting key internal security sites in and around Tehran City as well as Kurdish-populated areas of northwestern Iran. These targets range from regional Law Enforcement Command (LEC) and Islamic Revolutionary Guards Corps (IRGC) headquarters to local police stations in areas that have been hotspots for unrest.
4. The combined forces have continued to strike the IRGC Ground Forces’ special forces and rapid response units in southwestern Iran. The combined force has not yet struck other IRGC or Artesh special forces bases or infrastructure at scale across the country. The internet shutdown may be affecting ISW-CTP’s ability to observe these strikes, however.
5. US President Donald Trump said on March 5 that he would support Kurdish forces conducting an offensive into Iran. Trump told Reuters that he would be “all for” a Kurdish offensive into Iran but did not commit to supporting the operation with airstrikes. Senior Iraqi Kurdish officials have denied that Kurdish forces are deploying or planning to deploy into Iran, however.
6. US President Donald Trump told Western media on March 5 that the United States must be involved in the process of selecting a new supreme leader in Iran. Trump stated that he will not accept a new Iranian leader who will continue former Supreme Leader Ali Khamenei’s policies.
7. The Artesh and Iranian state media claimed that Iranian forces carried out drone strikes against US forces at Camp Buehring, Kuwait, on March 5. The Iranian Artesh separately launched drone strikes that targeted a US base in Erbil, Iraq, on March 5, according to Iranian media.

## Toplines

**The US and Israeli combined force has advanced to the next phase of their campaign, which will focus on targeting Iranian defense industrial assets, especially missile production facilities.[1]** The first phase of the campaign suppressed Iranian air defenses, decapitated its command and control, and limited its ability to retaliate with ballistic missiles and drones.[2] US Central Command (CENTCOM) Commander Admiral Brad Cooper and US Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth held a press conference on March 5 to give an update on the state of the campaign.[3] Cooper said that the combined force achieved air dominance over Iran, which will enable the force to reach targets deep inside Iran.[4] Air dominance is when air power creates the conditions that allow joint forces to operate with “impunity” throughout the adversary‘s battlespace.[5] Cooper added that Iranian ballistic missile attacks have decreased by 90 percent since the war began, and drone attacks are down 83 percent.[6] US Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth indicated during the press conference that the United States and Israel will expand offensive air operations as the war continues.[7] CENTCOM has reportedly requested additional military intelligence officers from the Department of Defense to support CENTCOM operations against Iran until at least mid-June 2026 and potentially through September 2026, according to a notification obtained by Politico on March 4.[8]

Israeli officials echoed these points on March 5. Israeli Chief of Staff Lieutenant General Eyal Zamir announced on March 5 that Israel is transitioning to the “next stage” of the campaign, transitioning from establishing air superiority and destroying Iranian ballistic missile systems to targeting other unspecified Iranian military capabilities and the Iranian “regime’s foundations.”[9]

**The Israel Defense Forces (IDF) has continued strikes targeting Iranian ballistic missile infrastructure in order to degrade Iranian missile capabilities.** The IDF reported on March 5 that Israeli aircraft had struck hundreds of missile launch sites across Iran and rendered over 300 ballistic missile launchers inoperable since February 28.[10] The IDF added that Israeli aircraft struck dozens of ballistic missile launchers as part of a wave of strikes on approximately 200 targets in western and central Iran.[11] These strikes targeting missile capabilities likely reduce the Iranian ability to conduct large-scale missile barrages by destroying launchers and destroying infrastructure required to prepare and fire missiles.

The IDF has also continued striking Iranian air defense systems in order to maintain air superiority over Iran. IDF Chief of Staff Major General Eyal Zamir stated on March 5 that Israeli pilots have conducted approximately 2,500 strikes and used over 6,000 munitions during the campaign.[12] Degrading Iranian air defenses allows US and Israeli aircraft to operate with less risk and greater freedom of operation over Iranian airspace. Zamir added that the IDF has destroyed around 80 percent of Iran’s air defense systems.[13] Israeli aircraft struck an Iranian air defense system in Esfahan Province on March 4.[14] The combined forces separately targeted radar systems near Kish Island in the Persian Gulf on March 5.[15] Anti-regime media previously reported strikes on radar infrastructure on Kish Island on March 3 and February 28.[16]

**The IDF issued an evacuation warning on March 5 for the Abbas Abad Industrial Zone and Shenzar Industrial Zone in Pakdasht, Tehran Province, likely in preparation to target Iran’s defense industrial base.[17]** The Abbas Abad Industrial Zone hosts companies linked to Iran’s defense industrial base, including Asre Sanat Eshragh Company, which has procured aluminum alloy products for entities owned or controlled by the Iranian Ministry of Defense and Armed Forces Logistics (MODAFL).[18] Asre Sanat Eshragh has supplied aluminum alloys to Iran Electronics Industries (IEI) and the Iran Aviation Industries Organization (IAIO) since at least 2016.[19] The United States sanctioned Iran Electronics Industries (IEI) and the Iran Aviation Industries Organization (IAIO), both MODAFL-controlled defense industrial entities involved in Iranian military and missile programs, in August 2019.[20] Iran often uses industrial zones to support its military and defense industrial base, including the procurement and production of ballistic missile components and drones. [21] The IDF also struck hangars and bunkers in a restricted area of the Artesh Shahid Farashahi ammunition depot in Manzariyeh, Qom Province, likely as part of its ongoing campaign to degrade Iranian military logistics and weapons storage infrastructure.[22]

![](https://understandingwar.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/03/Artesh-Shahid-Farashahi-Ammunition-Depot-March-5-2026.gif "Artesh Shahid Farashahi Ammunition Depot, March 5, 2026")

Satellite imagery from March 4 confirms that the combined force struck the Parchin Military Complex east of Tehran for the second time since February 28 and damaged several buildings.[23] Iran has used the Parchin complex to develop and manufacture advanced munitions, including drones and missiles.[24] The Parchin complex also played a key role in Iran’s pre-2003 nuclear weapons program. The IDF struck the Parchin complex in June 2025.[25]

![](https://understandingwar.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/03/Parchin-Military-Complex-March-5-2026.gif "Parchin Military Complex, March 5, 2026")

**The combined force continues to conduct airstrikes targeting key internal security sites in and around Tehran City as well as Kurdish-populated areas of northwestern Iran.** These targets range from regional Law Enforcement Command (LEC) and Islamic Revolutionary Guards Corps (IRGC) headquarters to local police stations in areas that have been hotspots for unrest. Open-source intelligence (OSINT) analysts reported that combined force airstrikes targeted and damaged two key bases for IRGC Ground Forces Sarallah Headquarters on March 5, including the Mohammad Rasulullah Security Branch’s Imam Reza Unit in Tehran City’s northwestern suburbs and the 7th Tehran Municipality Region’s Motahari Basij Resistance Regional Base in central Tehran.[26] Basij security units are trained and equipped to suppress urban protests and conduct anti-riot operations and intimidate and arrest protesters under IRGC direction.[27] An OSINT analyst confirmed airstrikes at an unspecified IRGC base in Parand town in southwestern Tehran Province on March 5.[28] ISW-CTP was unable to confirm the location of the base, however.

OSINT analysts reported that combined forces struck at least two IRGC Ground Forces bases in Harsin and Ravansar, Kermanshah Province, in northwestern Iran on March 5.[29] Commercially available imagery and an Israeli open-source intelligence account confirmed that the combined force also conducted airstrikes targeting an IRGC Intelligence Building and IRGC Ground Forces Nabi Akram Operational Division in Kermanshah City, Kermanshah Province, on March 5.[30] OSINT analysts confirmed airstrikes on March 4 that targeted IRGC sites in Baneh, Kurdistan Province, and struck the IRGC Ground Forces 15th Khordad Base, as well.[31] OSINT analysts published footage of airstrikes in Kashan, Esfahan, on March 5, in which citizens claimed that the target was a civilian sports complex that the IRGC had been using.[32]

![](https://understandingwar.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/03/IRGC-Intelligence-Building-in-Kermanshah-March-5-2026.gif "IRGC Intelligence Building in Kermanshah, March 5, 2026")

Iranian diaspora media and an OSINT analyst posted footage of airstrikes at two LEC command centers in Qom and Shahriar towns near Tehran City, and two local police stations in northern Tehran City and in southern Tehran Province.[33] Commercially available satellite imagery confirmed that another struck the LEC West Azerbaijan Province headquarters in Urmia, West Azerbaijan, in northwestern Iran. OSINT analysts on March 5 also reported airstrikes damaged local police stations in Kermanshah city in recent days and Esfahan City on March 5.[34]

[[![Map Thumbnail](https://understandingwar.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/03/Iran-Internal-Security-March-5-2026-4-PM-1-scaled.webp)](https://understandingwar.org/map/us-and-israeli-strikes-against-internal-security-between-march-4-at-400-pm-et-and-march-5-2026-at-400-pm-et/)](https://understandingwar.org/map/us-and-israeli-strikes-against-internal-security-between-march-4-at-400-pm-et-and-march-5-2026-at-400-pm-et/ "US and Israeli Strikes Against Internal Security Between March 4 at 4:00 PM ET and March 5, 2026 at 4:00 PM ET")

[[![Map Thumbnail](https://understandingwar.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/03/Tehran-Internal-Security-March-5-2026-4-PM-scaled.webp)](https://understandingwar.org/map/israeli-strikes-targeting-iranian-irgc-headquarters-and-other-military-institutions-in-tehran-between-march-4-2026-at-400-pm-et-and-march-5-2026-at-400-pm-et/)](https://understandingwar.org/map/israeli-strikes-targeting-iranian-irgc-headquarters-and-other-military-institutions-in-tehran-between-march-4-2026-at-400-pm-et-and-march-5-2026-at-400-pm-et/ "Israeli Strikes Targeting Iranian IRGC Headquarters and Other Military Institutions in Tehran Between March 4, 2026 at 4:00 PM ET and March 5, 2026 at 4:00 PM ET")

**The combined forces have continued to strike the IRGC Ground Forces special forces and rapid response units in southwestern Iran.** Commercially available satellite imagery from March 5 showed airstrike damage at the IRGC 3rd Hazrat‑e Mehdi Ranger Brigade base, which is in Khuzestan Province, southwestern Iran. The 3rd Hazrat-e Mehdi is a rapid response force. An OSINT analyst separately reported on March 3 that an airstrike targeted the Fajr Division base in Shiraz City, Fars Province.[35] The Fajr Division is a special forces unit. The combined force has not yet struck other IRGC or Artesh special forces bases or infrastructure at scale across the country. The internet shutdown may be affecting ISW-CTP’s ability to observe these strikes, however.[36] The internet shutdown in Iran is very likely restricting the Iranian people‘s efforts to post images and videos from within the country.[37]

An Israeli OSINT analyst assessed that airstrikes in Tabriz, East Azerbaijan, on March 5 targeted either the Islamic Republic of Iran Broadcasting (IRIB) broadcast station or a radio repeater.[38] The Iranian regime uses IRIB facilities to distribute regime propaganda.[39] The combined force previously targeted at least four IRIB facilities in Tehran and Kurdistan Province since the start of the war.[40]

![](https://understandingwar.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/03/3rd-Hazrat-e-Mehdi-Ranger-Commando-Brigade-Base-March-3-2026.gif "3rd Hazrat-e Mehdi Ranger-Commando Brigade Base, March 3, 2026")

## Insurgency

**US President Donald Trump said on March 5 that he would support Kurdish forces conducting an offensive into Iran.[41]** Trump told Reuters that he would be “all for” a Kurdish offensive into Iran but did not commit to supporting the operation with airstrikes.[42] Some Western media outlets have reported that Trump is in contact with Iraqi and Iranian Kurdish leaders to encourage them to foment an uprising in Iran.[43] Senior Iraqi Kurdish officials have denied that Kurdish forces are deploying or planning to deploy into Iran, however.[44] Iranian forces have continued to attack Iraqi Kurdish bases in Erbil with drones. Regime-affiliated media reported on March 5 that Iranian forces struck the headquarters of an Iraqi Kurdish militia.[45] Iranian security forces claimed that Iran’s northwestern and southeastern borders are fully secure. The IRGC Hamzeh Seyyed ol Shohada Operational Base in Urmia, West Azerbaijan, stated that Iran’s northwest border is “in complete security.”[46] The governor of Dehloran, Ilam Province, denied any reports of Kurdish attacks on March 5.[47]

Sistan and Baluchistan Province’s deputy LEC commander said on March 5 that “terrorist activity” there had increased.[48] The Mobarizoun Popular Front (MPF)–a coalition of anti-regime Baloch militias–announced on March 3 that it attacked a local LEC commander in Zahedan, Sistan and Baluchistan Province, marking the first claimed MPF attack since the war began on February 28.[49]

## **Supreme Leader Succession**

**US President Donald Trump told Western media on March 5 that the United States must be involved in the process of selecting a new supreme leader in Iran.[50]** The combined US-Israeli force killed Iranian Supreme Leader Ali Khamenei on February 28.[51] The Iranian constitution stipulates that the president, the judiciary chief, and a member of the Guardian Council will take over the responsibilities of the Supreme Leader until Iran’s Assembly of Experts convenes to select a new leader.[52] The Iranian Expediency Discernment Council appointed Guardian Council member Ali Reza Arafi as the third member of the Leadership Council on March 1.[53] The Assembly of Experts has not announced a new supreme leader at the time of this writing. Trump told Axios that Khamenei’s son, Mojtaba Khamenei, is the most likely successor, but noted that he would not accept Mojtaba Khamenei’s appointment.[54] Trump stated that he will not accept a new Iranian leader who will continue former Supreme Leader Ali Khamenei’s policies.[55] There are other unconfirmed reports that the Assembly of Experts may select Mojtaba Khamenei as the next supreme leader.[56]

## Iranian Retaliation

**Iran continued to target US forces and facilities with drone attacks in the Gulf Countries and Iraq on March 5.** The Artesh and Iranian state media claimed that Iranian forces carried out drone strikes against US forces at Camp Buehring, Kuwait, on March 5.[57] An Iranian drone previously struck Camp Buehring on March 1.[58] An Iranian drone strike previously killed six US service members at a US base in Kuwait on March 1.[59] Iran last attacked a US base in the Gulf on March 4, when Iran struck al Udeid Airbase, Qatar, with a ballistic missile. The attack caused no casualties.[60]

The Iranian Artesh separately launched drone strikes that targeted a US base in Erbil, Iraq, on March 5, according to Iranian media.[61] The exact Artesh target in Erbil Province is unclear, but Iranian drone strikes have previously struck the US base at the Erbil International Airport and the al Harir base in Iraq.[62] A likely Iranian or Iranian-backed militia drone struck a power generator at the US-operated Sarsang oil field in Duhok Province, Iraq, on March 5, causing a fire and forcing the facility to close temporarily.[63] HKN Energy, a US company, operates the Sarsang Oilfield.[64]

**The IDF said that it expects Iranian ballistic fire at Israel to stop soon, which corresponds with Israeli media reports that Iran launched only “a handful” of missiles on March 5**.[65] Iran launched at least eight barrages, mostly targeting Northern and Central Israel, since ISW-CTP’s last data cutoff on March 4.[66] The eighth barrage included what appeared to be an Iranian ballistic missile with a cluster warhead.[67] An Iranian ballistic missile with a cluster warhead previously landed near Tel Aviv, Israel, on March 3, injuring at least 12 people.[68] Iran previously fired ballistic missiles with cluster warheads during the 12-Day War.[69] One ballistic missile landed in Moshav Bareket, in central Israel, on March 5, but did not cause casualties or material damage.[70] A second ballistic missile during Iran’s eighth barrage likely landed in Tel Aviv, Israel, but Israeli media has not confirmed the impact at the time of this writing.[71] A senior, unspecified IDF official told Israeli media that the IDF expects Iranian ballistic fire at Israel to stop soon, after the combined force destroys Iran’s remaining ballistic missile launchers.[72] The IDF also told Israeli media that the rate of Iranian ballistic missile fire continues to decrease and that Iran only launched a “handful” of missiles during the first three barrages on March 5.[73] Israeli media added that Iranian ballistic missiles have struck Israel 13 times since February 28.[74] This number likely does not reflect the two most recent impacts that ISW-CTP recorded on March 5, however.

[[![Map Thumbnail](https://understandingwar.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/03/Iranian-and-Axis-Retaliatory-Strikes-in-the-Middle-East-March-5-2026.webp)](https://understandingwar.org/map/iranian-and-axis-retaliatory-strikes-in-the-middle-east-between-march-4-2026-at-400-pm-et-and-march-5-2026-at-400-pm-et/)](https://understandingwar.org/map/iranian-and-axis-retaliatory-strikes-in-the-middle-east-between-march-4-2026-at-400-pm-et-and-march-5-2026-at-400-pm-et/ "Iranian and Axis Retaliatory Strikes in the Middle East Between March 4, 2026 at 4:00 PM ET and March 5, 2026 at 4:00 PM ET")

**Iran conducted numerous drone attacks and some ballistic missile attacks targeting Arab Gulf countries on March 5. Iranian drone attacks appear to primarily target the UAE.**

The Emirati Defense Ministry said that it:

- Intercepted six of seven detected Iranian ballistic missiles. The seventh missile landed in the UAE.[75]
- Intercepted 125 of 131 detected drones. Six drones landed in the UAE.[76]

The Qatari Defense Ministry said that it:

- Intercepted 13 of 14 detected ballistic missiles. The fourteenth missile landed in the sea.[77]
- Intercepted four of four detected drones.[78]

Saudi Arabia separately intercepted three Iranian cruise missiles and an Iranian drone on March 4.[79] Drone interception shrapnel also wounded several people near the Al Dhafra Air Base in Abu Dhabi, UAE.[80] The UAE Defense Ministry said Iranian strikes have killed 3 people and wounded 94 others since February 28.[81]

Data from the Emirati Defense Ministry shows that Iran has consistently launched over 100 drones per day at the UAE over the past four days.

![Iranian Launches at the UAE](https://understandingwar.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/03/Iranian-Launches-at-the-UAE-1024x768.webp)

**Iranian drone and ballistic missile attacks on Gulf energy infrastructure have continued to disrupt the regional energy industry.** At least two Iranian ballistic missiles struck the Bahrain Petroleum Company’s (BAPCO) refinery near Ma’ameer, Bahrain, on March 5, which started a fire at the facility.[82] An Iranian ballistic missile or drone also struck an undetermined area of Doha, Qatar, on March 5.[83] Saudi Arabia has shifted crude exports from Strait of Hormuz facilities to the Red Sea via pipelines in response to the conflict, but the Red Sea system can only partially offset the disruption, according to Kepler analysts cited by the *Wall Street Journal*.[84] Qatar recently shut down gas liquification on March 4 and will not return to normal production and export levels “for at least a month.”[85]

**The combined force has continued to strike targets at the Bandar Abbas Military Port as the force seeks to disrupt the Iranian navy’s ability to attack international shipping and US Navy vessels.** Commercially available satellite imagery captured on March 4 shows that the combined force struck multiple sites at the Bandar Abbas Military Port, Hormozgan Province. The Bandar Abbas Military Port is home to the IRGC Navy Headquarters, the IRGC Navy 1st Naval District, and the Artesh Navy Southern Forward Naval Headquarters.[86] The commercially available satellite imagery shows damage to a drone storage facility at the port for the IRGC Navy’s 1st Saheb ol Zaman Regional base. The IDF previously struck this drone storage facility during the 12-Day War.[87] The commercially available satellite imagery also shows damage to the Artesh Navy Factory dry docks and an unknown building located next to Iranian submarine docks. The unknown building may be associated with the Iranian submarine force due to its proximity to the submarine docks. Two Iranian Kilo-class submarines were undergoing repairs at the docks, and one Kilo-class sat idle according to commercially available satellite imagery captured on February 26.[88] The Kilo-class submarines are not visible on commercially available satellite data from March 4. ISW-CTP cannot assess whether the idle submarine moved before the conflict or whether they have been sunk. US CENTCOM also confirmed that the United States has struck Iranian submarines in the first 48 hours of the campaign, however.[89] The combined strike force has repeatedly hit Iranian vessels and facilities at the Bandar Abbas Military Port since February 28.[90]

![](https://understandingwar.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/03/Damage-at-Bandar-Abbas-Military-Port-and-Shahid-Bahonar-Port-March-5-2026.gif "Damage at Bandar Abbas Military Port and Shahid Bahonar Port, March 5, 2026")

**Iran is continuing to attempt to disrupt international shipping within the Persian Gulf as part of a broader effort to impose a cost on the Gulf states.** United Kingdom Maritime Trade Operations (UKMTO) reported on March 4 that an oil tanker, later identified as the Bahamas-flagged *Sonangol Namibe*, experienced a large explosion on the vessel’s port side 30 nautical miles southeast of Mubarak al Kabeer, Kuwait.[91] The *Sonangol Namibe’s* crew reported seeing a “small craft” leave the vicinity after the explosion.[92] The oil tanker reportedly experienced a hull breach but reported no fires and that the crew was unharmed.[93] This incident marks the northernmost attack against a commercial vessel in the Persian Gulf since the start of the conflict on February 28.[94] The IRGC Navy claimed on March 5 that it conducted “missile strikes” targeting a “US tanker” in the northern Persian Gulf.[95] The IRGC Navy stated that Iran will target US, Israeli, and European vessels if detected.[96] The IRGC Navy did not name the vessel it attacked, but it is likely referring to the attack on the *Sonangol Namibe*. Reuters reported on March 5 that initial assessments indicated that the vessel was attacked by an Iranian Unmanned Surface Vessel (USV).[97] Iran may have used USVs to attack the commercial vessel because USVs can inflict greater damage to vessels than unmanned aerial vehicles. The UKMTO previously reported that an unspecified actor, almost certainly Iran, launched two unmanned surface vessels (USV) at an unspecified vessel 50 nautical miles north of Muscat, Oman, on March 1.[98]

[[![Map Thumbnail](https://understandingwar.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/03/Kuwait-Tanker-March-5-2026.webp)](https://understandingwar.org/map/tanker-targeted-off-of-kuwait-as-of-march-5-2026-at-400-pm-et/)](https://understandingwar.org/map/tanker-targeted-off-of-kuwait-as-of-march-5-2026-at-400-pm-et/ "Tanker Targeted off of Kuwait As of March 5, 2026 at 4:00 PM ET")

**Iran is reportedly continuing to load and export goods to foreign markets, despite the war.** Reuters, citing commercially available ship tracking data, reported that two Iranian-flagged dry bulk carrier cargo vessels departed Iranian ports on March 5.[99] The two Iranian cargo vessels were reportedly headed to Kuantan, Malaysia.[100] Both vessels have previously been sanctioned by the US for supporting Iranian sanctions evasions.[101] The departures mark the first Iranian attempt to transport cargo to other nations via the Strait of Hormuz since the start of the war.[102] Commercially available ship tracking data from March 5 showed that at least one of the Iranian cargo vessels passed through the Strait of Hormuz and entered the Gulf of Oman on March 5. Commercially available satellite imagery from March 5 separately showed four oil tankers reportedly loading eight million barrels of crude oil at Kharg Island, Iran’s main oil export terminal.[103] Oil exports are Iran’s economic lifeline and are critical to sustain its economy and military.[104]

**Multiple European nations have agreed to send naval vessels to defend Cyprus from further drone attacks.** Italian Defense Minister Guido Crosetto said on March 5 that Italy, France, the Netherlands, and Spain would provide unspecified “naval assets” to defend Cyprus “in the coming days.”[105] Multiple French naval vessels are en route or have arrived at Cyprus.[106] French President Emmanuel Macron on March 3 ordered the French *Charles de Gaulle* aircraft carrier battle group to deploy to the Mediterranean.[107] The French military announced that the French frigate *Languedoc* arrived off the coast of Cyprus on March 5.[108] UK Defense Secretary John Healey announced on March 5 that the British air defense destroyer HMS *Dragon* will arrive off Cyprus “within the next couple of weeks.”[109] Iranian-made drones hit Cyprus on March 1.[110] Cyprus houses thousands of British soldiers and two British military bases.[111]

## Axis of Resistance Response

**Hezbollah claimed that the group conducted six attacks targeting IDF positions and forces in northern Israel and southern Lebanon since ISW-CTP’s last data cutoff at 8:00 AM ET on March 5.[112]** Hezbollah claimed it launched a guided missile and two rocket barrages targeting IDF forces in Jabal Balat, Tyre District, and Markaba, Marjaayoun District, respectively.[113] Hezbollah also claimed it conducted two separate attacks targeting the IDF Yaara barracks and the IDF Naftali Base in northern Israel.[114] Three Lebanese sources “familiar with the deployments” told Reuters on March 5 that Hezbollah ordered its Radwan Force to deploy to southern Lebanon to block Israeli tank advances shortly after Hezbollah launched its first attack against Israel on March 2.[115] It is unclear how many Radwan fighters have deployed to southern Lebanon at the time of this writing. The Radwan Force is Hezbollah’s elite special operations unit that Hezbollah, with Iranian support, built to conduct major ground attacks into Israel in the event of war.[116] The majority of the Radwan Force, which has approximately 2,000 fighters, moved north of Litani River after the November 2024 Israel-Hezbollah ceasefire agreement.[117] This Reuters report comes shortly after Hezbollah directly engaged Israeli forces for the first time in the war on March 5.[118]

**The IDF continued its “forward defense maneuver[s]” in southern Lebanon on March 5.** An Israeli media correspondent reported that the IDF reinforced its forces in at least ten strategic positions in southern Lebanon and is preparing to deploy additional battalions.[119] The IDF has mobilized approximately 20,000 reservists since the conflict began on February 28.[120] The IDF published footage of the 300th Regional Brigade (91st Division) operating in southern Lebanon.[121] The 300th Regional Brigade has previouslyoperated in southern Lebanon to dismantle Hezbollah infrastructure.[122] The IDF published footage of the 810th Infantry Brigade (210th Division) conducting operations in Mount Hermon, southern Lebanon, to “expose enemy infrastructure” and prevent Hezbollah fighters from establishing positions along the border.[123] The 810th Infantry Brigade was formed in September 2024 and conducted numerous raids targeting Hezbollah infrastructure during the Fall 2024 conflict.[124]

**The IDF has continued to conduct airstrikes targeting Hezbollah military sites in Lebanon to degrade Hezbollah’s ability to conduct retaliatory attacks against Israel.** IDF Spokesperson Brigadier General Effie Defrin reported that the IDF has struck over 320 Hezbollah targets in Lebanon since the conflict began on February 28.[125] The IDF struck over 80 Hezbollah targets in the past day.[126] The IDF conducted airstrikes targeting Hezbollah fighters, rocket launchers, and command centers, including a Unit 127 command center, in southern Lebanon and Beirut on March 4 and 5.[127] Unit 127 is Hezbollah’s aerial unit responsible for developing, producing, and launching attack drones.[128] The IDF confirmed that it killed the following individuals:

- **Zaid Ali Jamaa.[129]** Jamaa was a Hezbollah fire and artillery commander in southern Lebanon responsible for launching rockets, missiles, and drones at Israel. The IDF stated that Jamaa previously held several positions in Hezbollah, including the Hezbollah Khiam sector commander, and participated in fighting in Syria alongside the Assad regime.
- **Wasim Attallah Ali.[130]** Ali was a Hamas commander responsible for “training and exercises” for Hamas’ military wing in Lebanon. The IDF Navy struck and killed Ali in Tripoli, which marks the first IDF strike in Tripoli since the war started on February 28.

**The IDF issued evacuation warnings for residents in Beirut’s southern suburbs and several villages in the Bekaa Valley on March 5.[131]** Beirut’s southern suburbs and the Bekaa Valley have historically been Hezbollah strongholds.[132] The IDF warned that it would soon strike Hezbollah “activities” in these areas.[133] IDF airstrikes have largely been concentrated in southern Lebanon, with some limited airstrike waves in Beirut.

[[![Map Thumbnail](https://understandingwar.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/03/Lebanon-Strikes-and-Evacuations-March-5-2026-scaled.webp)](https://understandingwar.org/map/israeli-strikes-and-evacuation-warnings-in-lebanon-between-march-4-2026-at-400-pm-et-and-march-5-2026-at-400-pm-et/)](https://understandingwar.org/map/israeli-strikes-and-evacuation-warnings-in-lebanon-between-march-4-2026-at-400-pm-et-and-march-5-2026-at-400-pm-et/ "Israeli Strikes and Evacuation Warnings in Lebanon Between March 4, 2026 at 4:00 PM ET and March 5, 2026 at 4:00 PM ET")

**The Lebanese government has continued to take unprecedented steps to limit Hezbollah and Iran’s ability to operate in Lebanon.** The Lebanese government announced on March 5 that it will ban any IRGC activity in Lebanon and seeks to deport IRGC members from Lebanon.[134] Lebanese Information Minister Paul Morcos stated that the Lebanese cabinet ordered Lebanese security forces to prevent any IRGC members from conducting any security or military activities and to arrest them in preparation for deportation.[135] The government also decided that Iranian civilians will no longer have visa-free entry into Lebanon.[136] The government’s action against the IRGC comes after the government declared Hezbollah’s military and security activities illegal on March 2.[137] The government’s recent steps represent an inflection in Lebanese politics because no previous Lebanese government has taken such direct steps against Hezbollah or the IRGC.

**Unspecified forces landed in Anbar Province, Iraq, near the Saudi border on March 3, according to Iraqi media. Iranian-backed Iraqi militias have previously attacked regional US allies from this region of Iraq.[138]** The Iraqi Security Forces (ISF) Karbala Operations Command’s 41st Brigade responded to the reported landing site, according to Iraqi media, citing an ISF security cable.[139] The Iraqi Joint Operations Command (JOC) confirmed on March 4 that unknown forces conducted an airstrike and fired at the responding unit, killing one Iraqi soldier, injuring two others, and damaging two ISF military vehicles.[140] No actor has claimed responsibility at the time of this writing. Deputy JOC Commander Lieutenant General Qais al Muhammadawi said on March 5 that the Iraqi government asked the US-led international coalition for clarification.[141]

The reported landing site is near the border from which Iranian-backed Iraqi militias have launched drone attacks into Saudi Arabia and the United Arab Emirates since 2019.[142] Iranian-backed Iraqi militias threatened on March 2 to expand their attacks to regional countries that host US troops.[143]

**Iranian-backed Iraqi militia Harakat Hezbollah al Nujaba military adviser Abdul Qadir al Karbalai threatened on March 5 to target “the Zionist agent Barzani” as well as his family members and businesses if his militia attacks Iraqi security forces or Iranian-backed Iraqi parties.[144]** It is unclear to which Barzani family member Karbalai was referring.

**Iranian-backed Iraqi militias continue to attack US forces.** The Islamic Resistance in Iraq, which is a coalition of Iranian-backed militias, claimed on March 4 that it had conducted 29 unspecified operations with “dozens” of missiles and drones targeting “enemy” bases in Iraq and the region.[145]

**Iranian-backed Iraqi militias threatened to expand their attacks to target unspecified European forces if they join the US-Israeli strike campaign against Iran.** The Islamic Resistance in Iraq and a Harakat Hezbollah al Nujaba military adviser published separate statements on March 4, warning that any European country that joins the US-Israeli strike campaign will become a “legitimate target.”[146]

**The ISF likely seized at least three trucks carrying missile launch platforms and a drone in two provinces on March 4 and 5, respectively, to disrupt Iranian-backed Iraqi militias’ attacks on US forces.** A security source told Iraqi media on March 4 that the ISF Basra Operations Command seized two trucks containing missile launch platforms in Basra Province.[147] The ISF Eastern Salah al Din Operations Command seized a truck carrying a drone in Salah al Din Province on March 5.[148] The United States and Israel have also conducted several airstrikes targeting Iranian-backed militias across Iraq since March 1 to disrupt their attacks.[149]

## Other Activity

**Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy told Italian media on March 5 that Ukraine is willing to share its expertise countering Iranian Shahed drones with the US, European, and Gulf countries.[150]** Zelenskyy said that Ukraine has received requests from the US, European, and Middle Eastern partners to share Ukraine’s counter-drone expertise and experiences, particularly in countering Iranian-made drone types.[151] Zelenskyy said that Ukraine will provide its counter-drone expertise to countries that send their representatives to Ukraine.[152]

# Iran Update Evening Special Report, March 6, 2026

*Source: <https://understandingwar.org/research/middle-east/iran-update-evening-special-report-march-6-2026/>*

## Key Takeaways

1. Russia is reportedly sharing intelligence with Iran to support Iranian attacks against US forces in the Middle East, which highlights the deepening cooperation between two major US adversaries. Iran may not have regular access to high-quality satellite imagery, even from commercially available sources, and may be relying on Russia to get such imagery. Russian intelligence sharing, thus, may be supporting Iranian strikes on US military assets. Multiple people familiar with US intelligence stated that China may be preparing to provide Iran with financial assistance and missile components.
2. The US-Israeli combined force has continued strikes targeting Iranian ballistic missile infrastructure in order to degrade Iranian missile capabilities and ultimately destroy the Iranian ballistic missile program. US Central Command (CENTCOM) Commander Admiral Brad Cooper stated on March 5 that ballistic missile attacks from Iran have declined by roughly 90 percent since the strikes began. A campaign that destroys Iran’s ballistic missile launchers, stockpiles, and production facilities would accomplish the US stated objective to “raze or level Iran’s ballistic missile industrial base” and systematically dismantle Iran’s missile production capability.
3. The combined force is continuing to target Iran’s defense industrial base, especially facilities that support drone development. The IDF targeted the Shokouhiyeh Industrial Zone in Qom Province and the Esteghlal Industrial Zone in Tehran Province, as well as an ammunition production facility and an ammunition depot in Tehran and Fars provinces.
4. Iran has continued to retaliate against Israel, US bases in the Middle East, Gulf states, and other regional countries for the joint force strike campaign. Iran has launched at least six waves of ballistic missile attacks in the previous six days. The lower number of missile attacks further demonstrates the joint forces’ success in degrading Iran’s number of missile launchers.

## Toplines

NOTE: A version of the following text also appeared in The Institute for the Study of War’s March 6 Russian Offensive Campaign Assessment:

**Russia is reportedly sharing intelligence with Iran to support Iranian attacks against US forces in the Middle East, which highlights the deepening cooperation between two major US adversaries.** Three officials familiar with the intelligence on Russia’s support for Iran told the *Washington Post* on March 6 that Russia has given Iran the locations of US military assets, including warships and aircraft, since the US-Israeli February 28 strikes against Iran.[1] One of the officials characterized Russia’s provision of targeting information to strike US forces in the Middle East as a “pretty comprehensive effort.”[2] The *Washington Post* noted that analysts indicated that Russia’s reported intelligence sharing fits the pattern of Iran’s strikes against US command and control infrastructure, radars, and temporary structures. The Center for Naval Analyses (CNA) Principal Research Scientist Mike Peterson stated that Russia could be giving Iran high-quality satellite imagery with which Iran could identify what bases the United States and its allies are currently using and other information, such as the location of aircraft, intelligence stations, and logistics flows.[3] Iran may not have regular access to high-quality satellite imagery, even from commercially available sources, and may be relying on Russia to get such imagery. Prominent commercial publisher of satellite imagery, Planet Labs, enacted a policy on March 6, subjecting all new imagery collected over the Gulf States and adjacent conflict zones, excluding Iran, to a mandatory 96-hour delay before making it publicly available, and other companies have held policies to never release images of US or allied bases.[4] Russian intelligence sharing, thus, may be supporting Iranian strikes on US military assets.

China may be preparing to provide Iran with support, according to multiple people familiar with US intelligence on this issue.[5] The intelligence suggests that China may be preparing to provide Iran with financial assistance and missile components.[6] China has historically provided Iran with sodium perchlorate to support Iran’s ballistic missile program.[7] Sodium perchlorate is a chemical precursor for solid missile propellant. European intelligence sources told CNN in October 2025 that Iran received 2,000 tons of sodium perchlorate in several shipments that arrived in Bandar Abbas, Iran, from China.[8] These shipments came amid Iranian efforts to rebuild its ballistic missile program following the June 2025 Israel-Iran war.[9]

**Reuters reported on March 5, citing a source familiar with the matter, that US and Qatari officials are in talks with Ukraine to purchase Ukrainian interceptor drones to intercept Iranian Shahed drones.[10]** The source stated that parties are also discussing the transfer of Ukrainian technologies that ”listen out“ for incoming enemy drones and ”disrupt their communication signals,” likely referring to acoustic sensors and electronic warfare (EW).[11] Ukraine manufactures and relies on relatively cheap Shahed interceptor drones that cost only a few thousand dollars each to produce.[12] Ukraine has years of experience defending against almost nightly large-scale Russian drone and missile strike packages, which have included Iranian-origin strike drones much like those the United States and its allies currently face in the Middle East.[13] Ukraine also has a reported 90 percent interception rate of such drones.[14] *The Telegraph* reported on March 4 that the UK sent Ukrainian-trained British drone operators to the Middle East as of March 3 to defend British bases in the region, and that the UK plans to deploy more later in the week.[15] One Western official told *The Telegraph* that these British drone operators have experience working in Ukraine, where they learned about layering sensing, warning, and targeting drones.[16]

**US President Donald Trump said on March 6 that the United States would only accept Iranian “unconditional surrender.”[17]** Trump later told Axios that “unconditional surrender” could be a formal Iranian surrender.[18] Trump said that an Iranian ”unconditional surrender” could also be achieved when Iran can no longer fight against the United States ”because they don’t have anyone or anything to fight with.”[19] White House Press Secretary Karoline Leavitt told Fox that “unconditional surrender” is achieved when “Iran can no longer pose a threat to the U.S. and our troops in the Middle East.”[20]

**The US-Israeli combined force has continued strikes targeting Iranian ballistic missile infrastructure in order to degrade Iranian missile capabilities and ultimately destroy the Iranian ballistic missile program.** Iranian missile forces are already badly degraded. US Central Command (CENTCOM) Commander Admiral Brad Cooper stated on March 5 that ballistic missile attacks from Iran have declined by roughly 90 percent since the strikes began.[21] The IDF also estimated that Iran has around 100 to 200 missile launchers remaining.[22] Cooper added that the operation aims to “raze or level Iran’s ballistic missile industrial base” and systematically dismantle Iran’s missile production capability.[23] Cooper’s comments are consistent with the doctrinal definition of destroy, which is when an enemy force loses the physical means or will to fight. A destroyed force cannot function without being entirely rebuilt. A campaign that destroys Iran’s ballistic missile launchers, stockpiles, and production facilities would accomplish this military objective. Satellite imagery from March 4 to 5 shows strikes on multiple ballistic missile facilities, including the Imam Ali Missile Base in Khorramabad, Lorestan Province, the Heydar Karar missile base in Tehran Province, and a ballistic missile launch site in Zanjan Province.[24] Satellite imagery from March 6 also shows strikes on the Yazd Province missile base.[25] The Israel Defense Forces (IDF) stated on March 6 that it had struck over 400 targets in western Iran, including ballistic missile launchers, as part of efforts to reduce missile fire toward Israeli territory.[26] These strikes aimed to degrade Iran’s ability to launch ballistic missiles and limit the regime’s remaining launcher inventory.

**The combined force is continuing to target Iran’s defense industrial base, especially facilities that support drone development.** The IDF targeted the Shokouhiyeh Industrial Zone in Qom Province after it issued an evacuation warning on March 6.[27] The industrial zone hosts companies linked to Iran’s defense industrial base, including drone production networks.[28] The United States sanctioned a company with facilities in the industrial zone, Oje Parvaz Mado Nafar Company (Mado), in October 2021, for providing material support to the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) and producing engines used in Iranian unmanned aerial vehicles such as the Shahed-131 and Shahed-136.[29] Mado reportedly manufactures drone propulsion systems that have been reverse-engineered from foreign designs and has supplied engines and components to IRGC-affiliated organizations and other Iranian defense firms involved in weapons development.[30] The combined force also targeted the Esteghlal Industrial Zone in Tehran Province on March 6.[31] The industrial zone hosts firms linked to Iran’s defense industrial base, including Azmoon Pajohan Hesgar LLC, which the United States sanctioned in 2024 for ties to Iran’s Ministry of Defense and Armed Forces Logistics (MODAFL).[32] The combined force separately targeted an ammunition production facility and an ammunition depot in Tehran and Fars provinces on March 6, likely as part of an ongoing campaign to degrade Iranian military logistics and weapons storage infrastructure.[33]

[[![Map Thumbnail](https://understandingwar.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/03/Defense-Industrial-Strikes-March-6-20261.webp)](https://understandingwar.org/map/us-and-israeli-strikes-targeting-iranian-defense-industrial-sites-from-march-5-2026-at-400-pm-et-to-march-6-2026-at-300-pm-et/)](https://understandingwar.org/map/us-and-israeli-strikes-targeting-iranian-defense-industrial-sites-from-march-5-2026-at-400-pm-et-to-march-6-2026-at-300-pm-et/ "US and Israeli Strikes Targeting Iranian Defense Industrial Sites, From March 5, 2026 at 4:00 PM ET to March 6, 2026 at 3:00 PM ET")

![](https://understandingwar.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/03/Strikes-Against-Iran-Target-Types-February-28-to-March-6-2026-1024x621.webp)

## US and Israeli Air Campaign

**The combined force has continued to destroy Iranian air defense infrastructure in order to maintain air superiority over Iran.** The combined force targeted the Shiraz air defense center in Fars Province on March 6.[34] These strikes are consistent with the first phase of the campaign, which focused on suppressing Iranian air defenses. US CENTCOM Commander Admiral Brad Cooper stated on March 5 that US and Israeli forces have achieved air dominance over Iran, enabling the combined force to strike targets deep inside Iranian territory.[35] Air dominance is when air power creates the conditions that allow joint forces to operate with “impunity” throughout the adversary’s battlespace.[36]

**The combined force continued targeting the Iranian internal security infrastructure. The strikes targeted Law Enforcement Command facilities and Basij bases across Iran.** These organizations play key roles in suppressing protests and maintaining regime control, and the strikes will likely degrade the regime’s internal security apparatus if the combined force continues to strike these targets at scale throughout the country. The combined force also struck the Islamic Revolutionary Guards Corps (IRGC) Imam Ali Garrison in Sanandaj, Kurdistan Province, on March 5.[37] The garrison hosts the IRGC Beyt ol Moghaddas Provincial Unit and its 22nd Beyt ol Moghaddas Operational Division, which is responsible for internal security and counter-insurgency operations in Kurdistan Province.[38] The 22nd Beyt ol Moghaddas Operational Division falls under the Hamzeh Seyyed ol Shohada Operational Base, one of the IRGC Ground Forces’ regional operational commands headquartered in Urmia, West Azerbaijan Province.[39] The European Union sanctioned the Beyt ol Moghaddas Unit for its role in suppressing protests and committing human rights abuses against demonstrators in Kurdish-populated areas of western Iran.[40] The combined force has also struck other internal security infrastructures in Ilam, Kurdistan, Kermanshah, and West Azerbaijan provinces.[41] These strikes are concentrated in Kurdish-populated areas, where the regime maintains internal security forces to manage unrest and counter Kurdish militant activity.[42]

![Imam Ali Garrison, March 6, 2026](https://understandingwar.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/03/Imam-Ali-Garrison-March-6-2026.gif "Imam Ali Garrison, March 6, 2026")

**Israeli media, citing Israeli security sources, reported that the IDF targeted former Iranian Supreme Leader Ali Khamenei’s Deputy Chief of Staff for Political and Security Affairs, Ali Asghar Hejazi, in an airstrike in Tehran on March 6.[43] It is unclear if Hejazi was killed at the time of this writing.** The IDF confirmed on March 6 that it struck a senior Iranian official but did not specify who the official was or if they were killed.[44] Five people familiar with Khamenei’s decision-making process told Reuters in June 2025 that Hejazi is one of Khamenei’s closest advisers and is considered the “most powerful intelligence official” in Iran.[45] A February 2026 *New York Times* report similarly identified Hejazi as a member of Khamenei’s inner circle, citing multiple senior Iranian officials, including one affiliated with Khamenei’s office, and three IRGC members.[46] The reported targeting of Hejazi comes amid a broader Israeli decapitation campaign, which is likely disrupting Iran’s ability to effectively retaliate against the US-Israeli strike campaign. US-Israeli strikes since February 28 have killed several senior Iranian officials, including former Supreme Leader Ali Khamenei, former IRGC Commander Major General Mohammad Pakpour, and former Armed Forces General Staff Chief Major General Abdol Rahim Mousavi.[47]

[[![Map Thumbnail](https://understandingwar.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/03/US-and-Israeli-Strikes-in-Iran-Evening-of-March-6-2026-scaled.webp)](https://understandingwar.org/map/us-and-israeli-strikes-in-iran-between-march-5-2026-at-400-pm-et-and-march-6-2026-at-300-pm-et/)](https://understandingwar.org/map/us-and-israeli-strikes-in-iran-between-march-5-2026-at-400-pm-et-and-march-6-2026-at-300-pm-et/ "US and Israeli Strikes in Iran, Between March 5, 2026 at 4:00 PM ET and March 6, 2026 at 3:00 PM ET")

[[![Map Thumbnail](https://understandingwar.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/03/US-and-Israeli-Strikes-in-Tehran-March-6-2026-scaled.webp)](https://understandingwar.org/map/us-and-israeli-strikes-in-tehran-between-march-5-2026-at-400-pm-et-and-march-6-2026-at-300-pm-et/)](https://understandingwar.org/map/us-and-israeli-strikes-in-tehran-between-march-5-2026-at-400-pm-et-and-march-6-2026-at-300-pm-et/ "US and Israeli Strikes in Tehran, Between March 5, 2026 at 4:00 PM ET and March 6, 2026 at 3:00 PM ET")

**The combined force continues to degrade the Iranian Navy.** Central Command (CENTCOM) Commander Admiral Brad Cooper said on March 5 that US forces have destroyed over 30 Iranian naval vessels since February 28.[48] This includes the IRGC Navy’s IRIS *Shahid* *Bagheri* drone carrier, which CENTCOM destroyed on March 5.[49] CENTCOM previously struck the *Shahid Bagheri* on February 28 but did not disclose details of the strike.[50] The *Shahid Bagheri* was a South Korean-built container ship that the IRGC Navy converted into a drone carrier and commissioned in February 2025.[51] CENTCOM’s destruction of the *Shahid Bagheri* follows similar CENTCOM strikes on the IRIS *Dena* of the coast of Sri Lanka and the IRGC Navy’s IRIS *Shahid Sayyad Shirazi*, of the coast of Bandar Abbas, on March 3.[52] The Sri Lankan navy separately said it took control of the Iranian IRIS *Bushehr*, a Bandar Abbas-class auxiliary support ship, off the coast of Sri Lanka on March 6, after the *Bushehr* suffered an engine malfunction.[53] The Sri Lankan Navy said it would take the Bushehr into the port of Trincomalee in eastern Sri Lanka and would impound the ship until further notice.[54] The Sri Lankan navy also evacuated the crew of the *Bushehr* to the Welisara Naval Base in Colombo for medical evaluations.[55] It is unclear if the *Bushehr* had an engine failure and, if it did, what caused the failure. The Bandar Abbas–class auxiliary support ships are West German–built vessels designed to sustain Iranian naval groups during extended operations by transporting fuel, supplies, ammunition, and other stores to accompanying warships.[56]

**Iran is continuing to attempt to disrupt international shipping within the Persian Gulf as part of a broader effort to impose a cost on the Gulf states.** United Kingdom Maritime Trade Operations (UKMTO) reported on March 6 that unknown projectiles struck a tug boat assisting operations on the cargo ship *Safeen Prestige,* which Iran previously attacked, in the Strait of Hormuz.[57] The UKMTO reported that there are unconfirmed reports of casualties following the likely Iranian drone strike on the vessel.[58] A likely Iranian unmanned surface vessel (USV) struck the *Safeen Prestige* on March 4, which caused a fire in the engine room and forced the crew to abandon ship.[59] The likely Iranian drone strike on the tugboat is the 13th maritime incident in and around the Strait of Hormuz that the UKMTO has reported since the conflict began on February 28.[60]

[[![Map Thumbnail](https://understandingwar.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/03/Maritime-Attacks-Iran-War-March-6-2026.webp)](https://understandingwar.org/map/iranian-attacks-against-civilian-vessels-in-the-persian-gulf-as-of-march-6-2026-at-1200-pm-et/)](https://understandingwar.org/map/iranian-attacks-against-civilian-vessels-in-the-persian-gulf-as-of-march-6-2026-at-1200-pm-et/ "Iranian Attacks Against Civilian Vessels in the Persian Gulf, As of March 6, 2026 at 12:00 PM ET")

## Iranian Retaliation

**Iran has continued to retaliate against Israel, US bases in the Middle East, Gulf states, and other regional countries for the joint force strike campaign.** The Pentagon said on March 5 that Iran has launched over 500 ballistic missiles and 2,000 drones since February 28.[61] Iran has conducted five waves of ballistic missile strikes between March 5 at 4:00 PM ET and March 6 at 4:00 PM ET.[62] This is a decrease in missile attacks as compared to the eight missile waves Iran launched from March 4 at 4:00 PM ET and March 5 at 4:00 PM ET.[63] Iran has launched at least six waves of ballistic missile attacks per day in the previous six days.[64] The lower number of missile attacks further demonstrates the joint forces’ success in degrading Iran’s number of missile launchers. CTP-ISW assessed on March 5 that the joint force advanced to the next phase of its campaign, which will focus on targeting Iranian defense industrial assets, especially missile production facilities.[65]

**Iran launched missiles and drones at Kuwait, Saudi Arabia, Bahrain, the United Arab Emirates (UAE), Qatar, and Iraq since March 5.**[66] CTP-ISW recorded no impacts of projectiles in Qatar, Iraq, Saudi Arabia, or the UAE. The Saudi Defense Ministry announced it had intercepted four Iranian missiles and 10 drones on March 5 and 6, which is an increase in Iranian attacks on Saudi Arabia compared to previous days.[67] Iran continues to launch the highest number of attacks against the UAE.[68] CTP observed Iran launch a total of 112 drones and 9 ballistic missiles at the UAE since March 5.[69] The Bahraini Defense Ministry announced on March 5 that two Iranian drones struck two residential buildings and a hotel in Manama, causing material damage.[70] Geolocated imagery also shows damage to a radar for a US THAAD system at Muwaffaq Salti Airbase. An Iranian missile attack struck the airbase on January 28.[71]

![](https://understandingwar.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/03/Iranian-Launches-at-the-UAE-March-6-1024x768.webp)

## Axis of Resistance Response

**The United States and Israel continue to conduct attacks targeting Iranian-backed Iraqi militia positions in Iraq in order to deter the militias from retaliating against the United States and Israel.** A security source in Ninewa Province told Iraqi media on March 6 that a fighter jet struck a Kataib al Imam Ali headquarters south of Mosul.[72] The combined force also reportedly struck an additional Iranian-backed Popular Mobilization Forces site in Ninewa Province on March 6.[73]

[[![Map Thumbnail](https://understandingwar.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/03/US-and-Israeli-Strikes-on-Iraq-March-6-2026-scaled.webp)](https://understandingwar.org/map/us-and-israeli-strikes-on-iraq-between-march-5-2026-at-400-pm-et-and-march-6-2026-at-300-pm-et/)](https://understandingwar.org/map/us-and-israeli-strikes-on-iraq-between-march-5-2026-at-400-pm-et-and-march-6-2026-at-300-pm-et/ "US and Israeli Strikes on Iraq, Between March 5, 2026 at 4:00 PM ET and March 6, 2026 at 3:00 PM ET")

**Iranian-backed Iraqi militias continue to attack US forces.** The Islamic Resistance in Iraq, which is a coalition of Iranian-backed Iraqi militias, claimed on March 6 that it had conducted 27 drone and missile operations against “enemy bases” in Iraq and the region “in the past 24 hours.”[74] The Islamic Resistance in Iraq has claimed a similar number of attacks every day since February 28, but ISW-CTP remains unable to verify whether these attacks are occurring in reality. Likely militia rocket and drone attacks separately targeted Basra International Airport, an oil field in Basra Province, the former US Victory Base at Baghdad International Airport, the Camp Taji Iraqi military base, and the headquarters of a US oil company in Basra Province on March 5 and 6.[75] Iraqi air defenses intercepted the attacks at Baghdad International Airport and the US oil company headquarters.[76]

[[![Map Thumbnail](https://understandingwar.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/03/Iranian-and-Axis-Retalitory-Strikes-Evening-of-March-6-2026-scaled.webp)](https://understandingwar.org/map/iranian-and-axis-retaliatory-strikes-in-the-middle-east-between-march-5-2026-at-400-pm-et-and-march-6-2026-at-300-pm-et/)](https://understandingwar.org/map/iranian-and-axis-retaliatory-strikes-in-the-middle-east-between-march-5-2026-at-400-pm-et-and-march-6-2026-at-300-pm-et/ "Iranian and Axis Retaliatory Strikes in the Middle East, Between March 5, 2026 at 4:00 PM ET and March 6, 2026 at 3:00 PM ET")

**Iranian-backed Iraqi militias may be using front groups in an attempt to avoid US and Israeli strikes on militia positions and obfuscate culpability.** Two Iraqi militias, which appear to be front organizations for other Iranian-backed Iraqi militias, Saraya Awliya al Dam and Rijal Albas Alshadid, claimed attacks targeting Jordan’s Muwaffaq al Salti Airbase, the US Rumalyn Landing Zone, and an unspecified “vital target” in Jordan, as well as an unspecified “vital target” in Duhok, Iraqi Kurdistan.[77] A Middle East analyst reported that a joint force strike struck a headquarters of Kataib Sayyid al Shuhada, a member of the Islamic Resistance in Iraq, in Mosul, Ninewa Province, on March 6 after Kataib Sayyid al Shuhada’s failed attempts to conduct attacks under Saraya Awliya al Dam’s name.[78] Saraya Awliya al Dam also claimed on March 6 an attack on the former US Victory Base at Baghdad International Airport.[79] Kataib Sarqhat al Quds, formerly known as Ashabl al Kahf and long considered a front group, announced on March 5 that it operates under Harakat Hezbollah al Nujaba’s instructions.[80]

**Hezbollah claimed that it conducted seven attacks targeting IDF positions and forces in northern Israel and southern Lebanon since CTP-ISW’s last data cutoff at 8:00 AM ET on March 6.[81]** Hezbollah claimed that it launched a guided missile, rockets, and drones targeting IDF forces in Jabal Balat, Tyre District, and Markaba and Tal al Hamamis, both in Marjaayoun District, respectively.[82] Two of Israel’s five permanent positions in southern Lebanon that it has occupied since February 2025 are located at Tal al Hamamis and Jabal Balat.[83] Hezbollah also claimed that it launched rocket and drone attacks targeting three Israeli military sites and one town, all in northern Israel.[84] Hezbollah previously targeted all four locations during the October 7 War.[85]

Hezbollah is likely attempting to displace northern Israeli residents from their homes to place political pressure on the Israeli government to curtail its war effort. Hezbollah also released a statement in Hebrew on March 6 calling for northern Israeli residents to evacuate their homes and move five kilometers south of the Israel-Lebanon border.[86] Hezbollah’s attacks after the October 7 War imposed political challenges on the Netanyahu government by forcing the evacuations of northern Israeli residents.[87] Hezbollah’s efforts during the October 7 War were ultimately unsuccessful and led to the Israeli campaign that degraded Hezbollah and returned Israelis to their homes.[88] Israeli Defense Minister Israel Katz said on March 6 that no northern Israeli residents should have to leave their homes or communities.[89] Katz also said that the IDF has reinforced its units operating in Lebanon and has “significantly expanded to additional positions” in Lebanon.[90] Israeli Chief of Staff Lieutenant General Eyal Zamir met with IDF Northern Command commanders on March 6 and discussed reinforcing Israel’s northern border units. Zamir said that the IDF will ”not give up on disarming Hezbollah” in order to protect Israel’s northern communities.[91]

[[![Map Thumbnail](https://understandingwar.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/03/Lebanon-CoT-Hezbollah-March-6-2026.webp)](https://understandingwar.org/map/hezbollah-attacks-in-northern-israel-and-southern-lebanon-from-march-5-2026-at-400-pm-et-to-march-6-2026-at-300-pm-et/)](https://understandingwar.org/map/hezbollah-attacks-in-northern-israel-and-southern-lebanon-from-march-5-2026-at-400-pm-et-to-march-6-2026-at-300-pm-et/ "Hezbollah Attacks in Northern Israel and Southern Lebanon, From March 5, 2026 at 4:00 PM ET to March 6, 2026 at 3:00 PM ET")

**The IDF has continued to conduct airstrikes targeting Hezbollah military sites in Lebanon to degrade Hezbollah’s ability to conduct retaliatory attacks against Israel.** IDF reported that it has struck 500 targets in Lebanon since February 28.[92] IDF Spokesperson Brigadier General Effie Defrin said that the IDF has killed over 70 Hezbollah operatives since Hezbollah launched rockets at Israel on March 1.[93] The IDF targeted multiple Hezbollah and IRGC headquarters and military infrastructure in Beirut’s southern suburbs on March 6.[94] The IDF reported that it struck an IRGC Aerospace Force Headquarters and Hezbollah’s Shura Council Headquarters, Unit 7900 Headquarters, a Hezbollah financial unit headquarters, a Radwan Force command center, and a drone storage warehouse.[95] The Shura Council is Hezbollah’s supreme decision-making authority.[96] Unit 7900 is Hezbollah’s naval unit, which is tasked with conducting sea-based attacks against Israel and Israeli targets.[97] The Radwan Force is Hezbollah’s elite special operations unit that Hezbollah, with Iranian support, built to conduct major ground attacks into Israel in the event of war.[98] Hezbollah reportedly ordered its Radwan Force to deploy to southern Lebanon to block Israeli tank advances shortly after Hezbollah launched its first attack against Israel on March 2.[99] International media reported on March 6 that Israeli airstrikes landed near the Iranian Embassy in Beirut.[100] The IDF has not confirmed that it targeted the Iranian embassy at the time of this writing.  The IDF also targeted the office of Hamas’ fundraising head, Issam Hashan, in southern Lebanon on March 6, with Shin Bet support.[101] The IDF said that Hashan and Hamas’ fundraising operations have raised ” hundreds of millions of dollars” for Hamas.[102] It is unclear if the IDF killed Hashan in the strike.

[[![Map Thumbnail](https://understandingwar.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/03/Lebanon-CoT-IDF-March-6-2026-Evening.webp)](https://understandingwar.org/map/israeli-strikes-in-lebanon-between-march-5-2026-at-400-pm-et-and-march-6-2026-at-300-pm-et/)](https://understandingwar.org/map/israeli-strikes-in-lebanon-between-march-5-2026-at-400-pm-et-and-march-6-2026-at-300-pm-et/ "Israeli Strikes in Lebanon, Between March 5, 2026 at 4:00 PM ET and March 6, 2026 at 3:00 PM ET")

**IRGC members have reportedly begun to “flee” Lebanon amid recent Israeli and Lebanese pressure.[103]** Two senior Israeli defense officials and another source with knowledge of the situation told Axios on March 5 that “several dozen” IRGC officers have fled Beirut in the last 48 hours.[104] One Israeli defense official said that Israel expects IRGC members, who are primarily associated with the IRGC Quds Force, to continue to flee Lebanon over the “next several days.”[105] The Israeli defense officials said that a “small contingent” will remain in Lebanon to liaise between Iran and Hezbollah.[106] CTP-ISW cannot confirm the accuracy of this report. If IRGC members are leaving Lebanon, this report could indicate that Iran does not believe Hezbollah is willing or able to effectively respond to the US and Israeli campaign.

Both Israel and Lebanon have increased pressure on the IRGC in Lebanon in recent days.[107] Israel has targeted both IRGC personnel and facilities in Lebanon since the start of the campaign.[108] The IDF killed Chief of Staff of the IRGC Quds Force Lebanon Corps Reza Khazai, who also served as Hezbollah’s chief of reconstitution, on March 3, for example.[109] The Lebanese government has also recently increased pressure on the IRGC in Lebanon. The Lebanese government announced on March 5 that it will ban any IRGC activity in Lebanon and seeks to deport IRGC members from Lebanon.[110] No previous Lebanese government has taken such direct steps against Hezbollah or the IRGC.

**Three UNIFIL peacekeepers were injured in a missile attack from an unspecified actor in southern Lebanon.[111]** UNIFIL reported that three UN peacekeepers were severely wounded in a strike by an unspecified actor on a UNIFIL base in Qouzah, southern Lebanon.[112] The Ghanian Armed Forces reported that two of the casualties were Ghanian peacekeepers.[113] UNIFIL said that it had begun an investigation into the incident.[114]
